5.3. Prestressed Rod with Smooth Wave Reflection

 Analysis Type Implicit (Adagio) and Explicit (Presto) Dynamics

 Element Formulations Mean quadrature, Selective deviatoric, Q1P0, Fully-integrated
 Strain Incrementations Strongly Objective and Midpoint
 Material Models Hyperelastic, St. Venant-Kirchoff

 Verification Quantities One-dimensional Displacement and Stress fields
 Number of Tests 17
 Keywords dynamic, temporal integration, convergence, wave propagation

5.3.1. Brief Description

This test group examines the convergence properties of the code for a one-dimensional wave
propagation problem, considering both explicit (central difference) and implicit
(Hilber-Hughes-Taylor) temporal integration methods. The test problem consists of a linear elastic
rod that is prestressed and then gradually released on one end (i.e., the traction boundary condition
is defined by a havercosine). An objective of this test is to provide a verification test with
sufficiently smooth wave propagation to attain the optimal asymptotic rates of convergence. Note
that this problem stands in contrast to another test group with a non-smooth wave propagation for
which the optimal asymptotic rate of convergence can not be obtained for second order methods.

5.3.1.1. Functionality Tested

 • Temporal integration methods:
 – central difference method (explicit)
 – Hilber-Hughes-Taylor method (implicit)

 • The following element formulations, all with strongly objective strain incrementation:
 – eight-node hexahedron with the mean quadrature formulation (results labeled by
 meanq)
 – eight-node hexahedron with the selective deviatoric formulation (results labeled by
 sdev)
 – eight-node hexahedron with the Q1P0 formulation (results labeled by q1p0)
 – eight-node hexahedron with the fully-integrated formulation (results labeled by full)
 • The following element formulations, with a midpoint strain incrementation:

 280

 – eight-node hexahedron with the mean quadrature formulation (results labeled by
 meanq)
 • resolution of kinematic boundary conditions
 • prescribed tractions
 • analytic user functions
Note that the mean quadrature element with midpoint strain incrementation is only examined for
explicit temporal integration, where it is most appropriately applied.

5.3.1.2. Mechanics of Test

A one-dimensional rod is fixed on the left end, with a traction applied to the right end, and
quiescent initial conditions. The traction is released in a smooth manner that produces a wave
transition region between the stressed and unstressed portions of the rod. The traction release is
defined in terms of the havercosine function that is explained further below.
The rod is modeled with hex elements, and mesh refinement is only applied in the axial direction.
The analytical solution is based upon the assumptions of linear elasticity, so the prestressing
traction was defined to only produce initial strains of 10 micro-strain, minimizing the inherent
nonlinearity that exists in Sierra/SM (reference the Verification manual introduction for more
discussion).

5.3.1.3. Material Model

The material model used for this problem is the Hookean model implemented in LAMÉ [4]. This
is a hyperelastic model and as such stresses are obtained from an underlying stored strain energy
function or elastic potential. For this problem the elastic coefficients are selected to correspond to
the St. Venant-Kirchoff model where the 2nd Piola-Kirchoff stresses are given by

                                         S = 𝜆(𝑡𝑟E)I + 2𝜇E                                       (5.8)

Note that this model has the same form as linear elasticity, but the strain measure (E) used in this
relation is the Lagrangian or Green strain tensor (which is quadratic in the displacement gradients,
not linear). In the templated data file the Lamé constants were expressed in terms of Young’s
modulus and Poisson’s ratio. The selected properties were given as follows, two of which are
representative of steel.

 Table 5-20. Material model properties
 Young’s Modulus 𝐸 200 GPa
 Poisson’s Ratio 𝜈 0
 Density 𝜌 7860 kg/m3

Note that defining Poisson’s ratio to zero was done to constrain the problem to being purely
one-dimensional. Otherwise, the rod would expand and contract in the transverse directions,

 281

giving lateral inertia effects that produce dispersion—a more complex problem to address than
considered here.
The existence of the underlying potential function for hyperelastic models implies that the stresses
are path independent (in contrast to a hypoelastic model). With material path independence the
time step size used does not affect the accuracy in terms of integrating the constitutive relations;
the time step size only affects the accuracy of integrating the equations of motion.

5.3.1.4. Mesh: Spatial Discretization

The coarsest mesh uses 20 uniformly spaced elements along the length of the rod (longitudinally)
and one element in each of the transverse directions, thus having element aspect ratios of 1/2.
Mesh refinements double the number of uniformly spaced elements in the longitudinal direction
alone. Since this is a one-dimensional problem, all the gradients are in the longitudinal direction
and thus the extreme aspect ratios that occur do not affect the accuracy of the results.

5.3.1.5. Time Discretization

To examine the convergence of the temporal integration methods, we use uniform time steps over
a time period equal to the time for a wave to traverse the length of the rod once (i.e., the wave front
reaches the fixed end). Since the spatial convergence rate of the displacements is second order and
the highest order temporal integration method is second order, consistent refinement (for the 2nd
order methods) requires the time step to be halved each time the mesh is refined. For each mesh,
the time step size is the length of time that a wave takes to traverse half an element, thus
maintaining stability of the central difference method (by a factor of one-half the critical step size,
according to the element-based critical time step estimate).

5.3.1.6. Hilber-Hughes-Taylor Method—Parameter cases

The Hilber-Hughes-Taylor alpha method defines a family of methods defined in terms of three
parameters (𝛼, 𝛽, and 𝛾). Initially we consider two HHT cases in the temporal integration tests, in
part to illustrate the loss of accuracy that generally occurs when not choosing the parameters to
yield a second order method. Following the conditions listed in the user manual for constraints on
the values of the parameters [3], we define a second order method denoted as HHT (order 2). We
also examine a case where the parameter constraints that guarantee second order convergence are
not met and denote the resulting first order method as HHT (order 1). Note that these constraints
on the parameters are mentioned in the FEM text of Hughes [3], apparently as sufficient but not
necessary conditions for second order convergence. Nonetheless the case selected here that does
not satisfy the constraints is seen to give first order convergence. The two integration methods
examined here are defined by the parameters listed in the table below.
Note that the second order case has parameters consistent with the current default parameters of
the code. In the context of the current problem, we use the labels order 1 and order 2 to denote the
optimal rates of convergence of these methods, but as discussed below second order convergence

 282

 Table 5-21. Hilber-Hughes-Taylor parameters
 HHT Case 𝛼 𝛽 𝛾
                                 order 1      -0.1     0.3025   0.61
                                 order 2      -0.1     0.3025   0.6

is not attained in this problem for the HHT (order 2) method.
For comparison, we added the special case of the HHT method reduced to the classical
Newmark-beta method (𝛼 = 0). This was added once the HHT (order 2) method was found to not
yield second order convergence, an initially unexpected result that will be discussed below.

5.3.2. Expected Results

This problem can be solved analytically using the classical approach of first solving the underlying
eigenvalue problem, and then projecting the governing equations upon each eigenvector to obtain
uncoupled, linear, ordinary differential equations (and initial conditions) in terms of the principal
coordinates. Alternatively one could solve it using the Laplace transform method [2]. In either
case, the resulting infinite series solutions while exact, leave the dilemma of determining how
many terms in the series are needed to produce a sufficiently accurate reference solution.
Examination of the response given by a series solution of the simpler, discontinuous stress wave
propagation case, reveals that the mechanics is simpler than the mathematics mentioned above.
Examining the equation of motion of a rod one can write it in the familiar form of the wave
equation, in which the wave propagation speed (also called the bar velocity in this context) is
given by

 √︄
 𝐸
 𝑐= (5.9)
 𝜌

Let 𝑡 𝐿 = 𝐿/𝑐 denote the time required for the wave to traverse one length of the rod. Unlike the
case of the discontinuous stress wave, as noted above, we only consider a single traversal of the
wave along the rod. This is because we can examine the convergence properties of the different
temporal integration methods without the analytical exercise of mathematically describing the
wave reflections off the fixed and free ends.
This problem was constructed to give a smooth analog to the discontinuous stress wave
propagation test (solved first). We selected the form of the stress release wave to have the shape of

the rod length. Furthermore we defined the length over which this transition would occur, denote

 (5.14)
where 𝑥 𝑓 (𝑡) and 𝑥 𝑏 (𝑡) are given in Equations (5.11) and (5.12) above and 𝑢 𝐿 is the initial
displacement at the end of the rod due to the prestress. Applying the equations of motion to this
displacement field shows that it solves the governing equations of linear elasticity with a zero
body load, which follows from our definition of a stress transition region that propagates in time
(i.e., having a form 𝑓 (𝑥 − 𝑡𝑐)). As the stress transition region (i.e., stress release wave) moves it
delineates the rod into two other regions one which has nonzero strain energy and zero kinetic
energy and one that has zero strain energy and nonzero kinetic energy. Equivalently, on one side
of the wave front the rod is uniformly strained, and on the other side of the wave back the rod is
traveling as a rigid body at constant velocity. Thus when the wave passes completely through a
differential section of the rod its strain energy is converted to kinetic energy, or visa versa. As
such, equating the two energies yields the speed of a rod particle as

                                               𝑣 𝑝 = 𝑐𝜖                                            (5.15)

where 𝜖 denotes the initial strain in the bar. Let 𝜎0 denote the corresponding initial prestress in the
bar.
For the case of the discontinuous stress wave propagation, we considered the wave propagation in
four phases, each characterizing the wave traversing the length of the rod. For this problem we
simplify that and only consider the first phase which can be characterized as follows:
 • Phase 1—tensile stress relief wave traveling left from the free-end to the fixed-end,
 𝑡 ∈ (0, 𝑡 𝐿 )
 – Left of the wave front: 𝜎 = 𝜎0 and 𝑣 = 0
 – wave front and to wave back: 𝜎 transitions from 𝜎0 to 0 as a havercosine wave with 𝑣
 increasing from 0 to 𝑣 𝑝
 – Right of the wave back: 𝜎 = 0 and 𝑣 = 𝑣 𝑝 , rigid body motion to the left
Unlike the discontinuous wave propagation problem, for this problem both the spatial derivative
(strain) and temporal derivative (velocity) of the displacement field are continuous, and thus the
solution has the necessary 𝐶 1 -continuity used to prove the optimal rates of convergence.
This test group examines the rate of convergence by examining the 𝐿 2 -norm of the error in the
displacement and stress states at two times:
 1. 𝑡 = 𝑡 𝐿 /2—where the wave front is halfway down the rod, and
 2. 𝑡 = 𝑡 𝐿 —where the wave front is at the fixed end of rod.

 285

5.3.3. Verification Results

The emphasis of this test is to examine the convergence rates of the CDM and HHT methods when
a spatial approximation exists, and the solution is sufficiently smooth to attain the optimal
asymptotic rated of convergence. While for the actual test implementation, convergence of the two
states listed above is examined for each combination of temporal integration method and element
formulation, for brevity the results presented below are limited to those for state 1. Generally
because the predicted wave speed is in error for the numerical results, obtaining results in the
asymptotic range is facilitated by comparing states that occur earlier in time; for this problem, the
time duration effect on the convergence rates is most apparent for cases that exhibit first order
convergence.
Figures 5-18 through 5-21 show the relative errors in the displacement field versus element size
for the four temporal integration methods. Tables 5-22 through 5-38 give the corresponding rates
of convergence calculated from successive mesh refinements. The unexpected results for these
tests are the first order convergence of the displacement field for (1) the CDM when combined
with the selective deviatoric element, and (2) the HHT (2nd order) method. For the cases of the
HHT (2nd order) method, our leading suspect for the issue is the effect of the lumped mass matrix
approximation upon the asymptotic rate, but curiously the second order Newmark-beta method (a
member of the HHT family with 𝛼 = 0) yields second order convergence. To examine this further,
we solved the same problem in Sierra/SD which has a generalized HHT method; the
generalization consists of a different 𝛼 parameter for the inertia terms in the equations of motion.
To run the same case we had to hard-wire the coefficients in the code, which usually defines all the
parameters in terms of a single parameter, 𝜌. The results give further evidence, as Sierra/SD gave
first order convergence with a lumped mass matrix and second order convergence with a
consistent mass matrix.

For this simple problem the element formulation has little effect upon the results, the one
exception being the response of the selective deviatoric element combined with the CDM; for this
case the convergence rate of the displacements is reduced to first order and the displacement and
stress results are less accurate compared with the other element formulations. All element
formulations had less than about half percent relative error in the displacement field, even for the
coarsest mesh, and the relative error in the stress fields ranged to less than ten percent for the
coarsest meshes to less than one percent for the two finest meshes.

    0.0625              1.9779                           1.7076                              1.0172

 294

Summary of results: The objective of this test was to determine if the temporal integration
methods that have an optimal asymptotic rate of convergence of two, can realize that convergence
rate for a smooth stress wave propagation problem. The CDM exhibits its optimal asymptotic rate
of convergence (second order) for all hex-8 element formulations except the selective deviatoric
element; the cause of this element’s failure to attain second order convergence is as of yet unknown.
The HHT (2nd order) method did not attain its optimal asymptotic rate of convergence for any
element formulation, and yet the second order Newmark-beta flavor of HHT did attain the optimal
asymptotic rate. The following evidence suggests that the HHT (2nd order) method (which has 𝛼 ≠
0) does not attain its optimal asymptotic rate of convergence due to the lumped mass approximation
of the inertia contributions: (1) the method attained second order convergence for the discrete
spring-mass system, even when the springs were composed of individual brick elements, (2) the
method also exhibited first order convergence for a simpler problem (haversine traction application
to the rod without a prestress), and (3) running the same prestress problem in Sierra/SD yielded
first order convergence for a lumped mass approximation and second order convergence for a
consistent mass approximation. While an error quantification tests provides stronger verification,
it is appropriate to examine a linear elastic problem for which the theoretical determination of
the optimal asymptotic rates of convergence are based upon. Adjustment of the load level to be
relatively small allowed the inexact reference solution (relative to the basis of the code) to serve as
a reasonably accurate surrogate solution, yielding second order convergence rates accurate to a few
digits.

5.3.4. References

 1. Achenbach, J.D. Wave Propagation in Elastic Solids, New York, NY: Elsevier, 1975.
 2. Graff, K.F. Wave Motion in Elastic Solids, New York, NY: Dover Publications, Inc., 1991.

 4. Scherzinger, W.M. and Hammerand, D.C. Constitutive Models in LAMÉ. Sandia Report

For input deck see Appendix B.45.

6. POST-PROCESSING TESTS

The following tests verify post-processing capabilities. Tests are included to verify the capabilities
and that any errors in the output quantities are within expected bounds.

6.1. J-Integral

 Analysis Type Quasi-statics
 Element Type Hex8, Tet10
 Element Formulations Mean Quadrature, Composite Tetrahedron
 Strain Incrementation Strongly Objective

 Verification Category Discretization Error
 Verification Quantities Fracture Energy (J-Integral)
 Number of Tests 4
 Keywords J-Integral, User Output

6.1.1. Problem Description

This test checks that the numerical values for fracture energy, computed by the J-integral
capability in Sierra/SM, are approximately equal to known values. The problem is a single block
representing approximately an infinite plate with an elliptical crack in the center. The model
geometry is shown in Figure 6-1. The crack has a major radius of 0.2 and minor radius of 0.1, and
the plate has a length of 5, width of 5, and thickness of 2. See references [1, 2] for further
descriptions of the problem.
Multiple crack meshing and modeling techniques are evaluated and compared. In simple
geometries, like the one used in this test, the entire model can be easily modeled with a hexahedral
mesh throughout. This is one of the techniques evaluated here. In more complex geometries, a
tetrahedral mesh is desired for robustness, ease, and efficiency of meshing. However, the
J-integral capability requires hexahedral meshes due to current algorithmic restrictions. Thus, for
analyses involving tetrahedral meshes of a cracked geometry, the solution fields on the tetrahedral
mesh are transferred to a well-structured hexahedral mesh template of the crack, where then the
J-integral is evaluated. This crack template-based approach is illustrated in Figure 6-2. This is the
second technique evaluated here.

 297

 (a) The plate with the crack in the center (b) The elliptical crack

 Figure 6-1. Geometry of the plate with an embedded elliptical crack. The FE model is one-eighth of the
 plate (one-quarter of the crack) and uses x-, y-, and z-symmetry BC’s.

6.1.1.1. Boundary Conditions

The boundary conditions consist of pressure loading on the crack face, far-field kinematic
constraint, and symmetry. The crack face pressure is uniform spatially and ramps upwards over
time to a value of 1 × 105 . The end of the plate is fixed and only one-eighth of the plate is
modeled, with fixed normal-direction constraints on three symmetry surfaces.

6.1.1.2. Material Model

A (hypo-)elastic material model is used, with the parameters shown in Table 6-1.

 Table 6-1. Elastic Material Properties
 Young’s Modulus 𝐸 2 × 1011
 Poisson’s Ratio 𝜈 0.25

6.1.1.3. Feature Tested

This test verifies that the numerically evaluated J-integral values along the crack front remain
within 5.0 percent of the analytical solution when the peak pressure is applied. Some error is
expected due to minor violations of the analytical solution assumptions (e.g., small strain), and

 298

 Figure 6-2. Illustration of crack template-based approach for J-integral calculations.

small mapping errors are introduced for the cases involving transfers. The mapping errors are
known to converge to zero with mesh refinement.

6.1.2. Assumptions and Notes

This problem is assumed to be quasi-static, small strain, and linear elastic. The analytical solution
is derived from linear elastic fracture mechanics (LEFM). The configuration is assumed to
represent an elliptical flaw in an infinite plate with mode-I (𝐾 𝐼 ) loading.

6.1.3. Verification of Solution

The equation for the analytical solution at a point 𝑝 on the elliptical crack front is

                                            (𝐾 𝐼 ) 2 (1.0 − 𝜈 2 )

𝜎 is the far-field tensile stress, 𝑎 and 𝑏 are the major and minor radii of the ellipse, 𝐸 is the
Young’s modulus, 𝜈 is the Poisson’s ratio, and 𝜑 is the angle for 𝑝 shown in Figure 6-1.
Six test cases are evaluated and compared:
 1. "no transfer (UG Hex8)": Use a hexahedral mesh with the uniform gradient formulation to
 run the analysis and compute the J-integral.
 2. "procedural transfer - UGHex8 to UGHex8": Use a hexahedral mesh with the uniform
 gradient formulation to run the analysis, and then do a Sierra/SM procedural transfer onto a
 separate crack template mesh to compute the J-integral with uniform gradient hexahedral
 elements.
 3. "procedural transfer - CTet10 to UGHex8": Use a 10-noded tetrahedral mesh with the
 composite tetrahedron formulation to run the analysis, and then do a Sierra/SM procedural
 transfer onto the crack template mesh to compute the J-integral with uniform gradient
 hexahedral elements.
 4. "solution-control transfer - CTet10 to VoidHex8": Use a 10-noded tetrahedral mesh with the
 composite tetrahedron formulation to run the analysis, and then do an arpeggio
 solution-control transfer onto the crack template mesh to compute the J-integral with void
 hexahedral elements.
 5. "region transfer - UGHex8 to UGHex8": Use a hexahedral mesh with the uniform gradient
 formulation to run the analysis, and then do a "adagio region"-to-"J_integral region"
 transfer onto a separate crack template mesh to compute the J-integral with linear
 hexahedral elements.
 6. "region transfer - CTet10 to UGHex8": Use a 10-noded tetrahedral mesh with the composite
 tetrahedron formulation to run the analysis, and then do a "adagio region"-to-"J_integral
 region" transfer onto the crack template mesh to compute the J-integral with linear
 hexahedral elements.
The first three use cases represent common workflows using the adagio executable with and
without procedural transfers; the fourth use case represents a slightly more complex, but more
versatile workflow using solution control via the arpeggio executable; and the last two use cases
represent a robust, fully-automated workflow using specialized code capabilities for coupling a
Sierra/SM region with a J-integral region. The procedural transfers in adagio allow a single
one-way transfer from the sending mesh to the receiving mesh; this significantly complicates the
ability to calculate J-integral values at multiple time steps during an analysis. In contrast, solution
control and region-to-region coupling approaches allow transfers at any number of time steps, thus
facilitating calculation of the J-integral at multiple time steps during an analysis.
Figure 6-3 shows the computed J-integral values along the crack front for each test case, with
comparisons to the analytical solution. All computed values are within 5% of the analytical

APPENDIX A. Other Sierra/SM Verification Tests not in this Document

The following tables list verification tests that exist in the nightly Sierra/SM test suite, but have not
yet been formally added to this document. Each of these tests is run nightly and compares the
results of a Sierra/SM code run to an analytic result. Over time, more of these tests will be
included in this document with full documentation of code results and analytic solutions.
In addition to the verification tests, several thousand additional regression tests are run nightly
with the Sierra/SM code suite. These regression tests ensure that: different capabilities remain
functional when used together; the performance of the code does not degrade; all supported input
commands are handled correctly; and, code results of analyses that are too complex to have an
analytic solution still compare closely with known acceptable results.

B.1. Contact Force Balance

See Section 2.1 for problem description.
 begin sierra contact_force_balance

 end

       evaluate expression is "x <= 1.0 ? sin((pi/2.0)*x) : 1.0;"
 end

 begin material flubber
       density = 100.0

 youngs modulus = 30.0e6
 poissons ratio = 0.3

 end material flubber

 begin finite element model contact_force_balance1
 database name = contact_force_balance.g
 database type = exodusII

 material = flubber
 model = elastic
 end

 material = flubber
 model = elastic
 end

 end finite element model contact_force_balance1

 begin adagio procedure fred

 title Analysis of Hertz-Mindlin-Lubkin contact model

 begin function load

 begin function analytic_radius

 #expression variable: P = global load
       evaluate expression = " pow((0.75*50000000*x*1/68900000000),(1.0/3.0))"
 end

 begin function analytic_compression
 type = analytic
 expression variable: a = global analytic_radius
 evaluate expression = "(a*a)/1"
 end

 begin function ErrorCM
 type = analytic
 expression variable: ac = global analytic_compression
 expression variable: c = global compression
       evaluate expression = " (ac > 0.0) ? (abs( abs(c) - ac )/ac)   * 100.0 : 0.0 "
 end

 begin function XYradius
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(x)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(y)
 expression variable: dx = nodal displacement(x)

 314

 expression variable: dy = nodal displacement(y)
 evaluate expression = "sqrt((x+dx)^2+(y+dy)^2)"
 end

  begin function angle
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(x)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(y)
 expression variable: dx = nodal displacement(x)
 expression variable: dy = nodal displacement(y)
    evaluate expression = " ( atan2((y+dy),(x+dx)) < 0.0 ) ?
atan2((y+dy),(x+dx))*(180.0/pi) + 360.0 : atan2((y+dy),(x+dx))*(180.0/pi) "
  end

 begin function contact_radius
 type = analytic
 expression variable: cs = nodal contact_status
 expression variable: cnt = nodal contact_normal_traction_magnitude
 expression variable: radius = nodal XYradius
   evaluate expression = " ( cs > 0.9 && abs(cnt) > 0.0 && radius < 0.5 ) ? radius : 0.0"
 end

 begin function analytic_pressure

 expression variable: a = global analytic_radius
 expression variable: r = nodal XYradius
 expression variable: P = global load
   evaluate expression = "(r <= a) ? 1.5*P/(pi*a*a)*sqrt(1-(r/a)*(r/a)) : 0.0"
 end

 begin function contact_pressure

 expression variable: fx = nodal force_contact(x)
 expression variable: fy = nodal force_contact(y)
 expression variable: fz = nodal force_contact(z)
 expression variable: ca = nodal contact_area
   evaluate expression = " (ca > 0.0) ? (sqrt(fx^2+fy^2+fz^2)/ca) : 0.0"
 end

B.4. Hertz Cylinder-Cylinder Contact—Convergence Test

See Section 2.4 for problem description.
   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # HertzContactOf2Cylinders Test
 #
   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # Aprepro default mesh value to help FCT
   #{mesh="2"}
 #
 # Aprepro variable settings that are passed in from the test script
 # shape: {shape}
 # mesh: {mesh}
 # elem_topo: {elem_topo}
 # formulation: {formulation}
 # Formulation flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # mean_quad: {mean_quad}
 # selective_dev: {selective_dev}
 # strain_incrementation: {strain_incrementation}
 # material_model: {material_model}
 # delta_tolerance: {delta_tolerance}
 # Contact algorithm flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # node_face: {node_face}
 # face_face: {face_face}
 #
 # Aprepro variable settings that are common to all analyses
 # termination_time: {termination_time = 0.02}
 # Number of steps that works with tangent pre is 10
 # number_steps: {number_steps = 5}
 # epsilon_time is an offset from the termination_time used to ignore all results
 # except those associated with the final time step.
 # epsilon_time: {epsilon_time = termination_time/(number_steps*2)}
   # Ey: {Ey = 100000.0}
   # nu: {nu = 0.2}
   # R: {R = 4.0}
 # cylinder_length AKA disk thickness
 # cylinder_length: {cylinder_length = 0.1/(2^(mesh-1))}
 #
   #---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #

 begin sierra Hertz2Cylinders

   # Functions -----------------------------------------------------------

 # Linear time function for displacement
 begin function delta

 expression variable: time = global time
 evaluate expression = "time"
 end function delta

   # material data -------------------------------------------------------

 begin material Mat_1
 density = 1.0

 begin parameters for model {material_model}
 youngs modulus = {Ey}
 poissons ratio = {nu}
 end parameters for model {material_model}
 end material Mat_1

 begin material Mat_2

 325

 density = 1.0

 begin parameters for model {material_model}
 youngs modulus = {Ey}
 poissons ratio = {nu}
 end parameters for model {material_model}
 end material Mat_2

# section data (required to create non-default hex elements) ---------

 # strain incrementation = {strain_incrementation}
 formulation = {formulation}
 {Ifdef(selective_dev)}
 deviatoric parameter = 0.5
 {Else}
 # Undefined selective_dev
 {Endif}

# FE model ------------------------------------------------------------

 begin finite element model Hertz_mesh
 Database Name = {shape}_{elem_topo}_{mesh}.g
 Database Type = exodusII

 material = Mat_1
 model = {material_model}
 section = solid_section
 {Ifdef(mean_quad)}
 linear bulk viscosity = 0.06
 quadratic bulk viscosity = 1.20
 hourglass stiffness = 0.05
 hourglass viscosity = 0.0
 {Else}
 # Undefined mean_quad
 {Endif}
 end block defaults

 material = Mat_2

 end finite element model Hertz_mesh

# procedure data ------------------------------------------------------

 begin adagio procedure Hertz_Proc

 begin time stepping block linear_time
 start time = 0.0
 begin parameters for adagio region Hertz_Region
 time increment = {termination_time/number_steps}
 end parameters for adagio region Hertz_Region
 end time stepping block linear_time
 termination time = {termination_time}

# region data ::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::::

 begin adagio region Hertz_Region
 use finite element model Hertz_mesh

# BC data ..............................................................

 # Prescribed through-plane displacements to give plane-strain conditions

 surface = plusZ_surface minusZ_surface
 component = z
 end

 # Prescribed vertical displacement on the bottom surface of bottom 1/2 cylinder

 node set = btm_flat
 component = Y
 function = delta
 scale factor = 1.0
 end

 # Prescribed vertical displacement on the top surface of top 1/2 cylinder

 node set = top_flat
 component = Y
 function = delta
 scale factor = -1.0
 end

 # prescribed displacements to prevent remaining rigid body motions
 # mid-points on top and bottom surfaces

 node set = top_z_line btm_z_line
 component = x

# Contact parameters ...................................................

 search = dash
 enforcement = al

 {Ifdef(face_face)}
 skin all blocks = on
 begin interaction cyl2cyl
 surfaces = btm_block top_block
 # this is performing frictionless contact by default.
 end interaction cyl2cyl
 {Endif}

 {Ifdef(node_face)}
 contact node set top_node_set contains top_cylinder_nodes
 contact surface btm_surface contains btm_cylinder_sides
 search = dash
 begin interaction cyl2cyl
 side A = btm_surface
 side B = top_node_set
 end interaction cyl2cyl
 {Endif}

 type = scale_factor
 scale factor = 0.0 0.0
 end

B.5. Mindlin Cylinder-Cylinder Contact—Convergence Test

   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # MindlinContactOf2Cylinders Test

 # This first version of the test uses a single procedure but two time blocks.
 # It is sufficient if the friction forces developed during the normal relative
 # displacment do not have significant tangent components.
 #
   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # Aprepro default mesh value to help FCT
   #{mesh="2"}
 #
 # Aprepro variable settings that are passed in from the test script
 # shape: {shape}
 # mesh: {mesh}
 # elem_topo: {elem_topo}
 # formulation: {formulation}
 # Formulation flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # mean_quad: {mean_quad}
 # selective_dev: {selective_dev}
 # strain_incrementation: {strain_incrementation}
 # material_model: {material_model}
 # delta_tolerance: {delta_tolerance}
 # Solver flag: 0~off, 1~on
 # tangent_pre: {tangent_pre}
 # Contact algorithm flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # node_face: {node_face}
 # face_face: {face_face}
 # Torque force flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # reaction_forces: {reaction_forces=0}
 # contact_forces: {contact_forces=1}
 #
 # Aprepro variable settings that are common to all analyses
 # preload_termination_time: {preload_termination_time = 0.02}
 # shear_time_increment: {shear_time_increment = 0.01}
 # shear_termination_time: {shear_termination_time =
 preload_termination_time + shear_time_increment}
 # preload_number_steps: {preload_number_steps = 20}
 # shear_number_steps: {shear_number_steps = 20}
 # epsilon_time is an offset from the termination_time used to ignore all results
 # except those associated with the final time step.
 # epsilon_time: {epsilon_time = shear_time_increment/(shear_number_steps*2)}
 #
   # Ey: {Ey = 100000.0}
   # nu: {nu = 0.2}
   # R: {R = 4.0}
 # cylinder_length AKA disk thickness
 # cylinder_length: {cylinder_length = 0.1/(2^(mesh-1))}

   #---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #

 begin sierra Mindlin2Cylinders

   # Functions -----------------------------------------------------------

 # Linear time function (during first time period) for normal displacement
 begin function delta_preload

 expression variable: time = global time
 evaluate expression = "(time<{preload_termination_time}) ? time : {preload_termination_time}"
 end function delta_preload

 330

 # Linear time function (during second time period) for lateral (shear) displacement
 begin function delta_shear

 expression variable: time = global time
 evaluate expression = "(time>{preload_termination_time}) ? time-{preload_termination_time} : 0.0"
 end function delta_shear

 {Else}
 # Undefined selective_dev

# BC data ..............................................................

 scale factor = -1.0

 # prescribed displacements to prevent remaining rigid body motions
 # mid-points on top and bottom surfaces

 small number of iterations = 15

 end
 {Else}

 preconditioner = elastic
 {Endif}
 end cg

# Output data ........................................................

 end

 # use reaction force on bottom of bottom cylinder
 compute global Qt as sum of nodal reaction(x)

 # For mesh 2: first "circular" mesh ring is in contact at a load level of 0.022 (to 2 digits)

  node set = NodeFacet
  # use reaction force on top of top sphere
  compute global P as sum of nodal reaction(y)
  # First set of user outputs is for the Hertz part of the solution -- the preload
  compute global delta_analyt from expression "3^(2/3)*((-1+{nu}^2)^2*P^2/({Ey}^2*{R}))^(1/3)/(2^(1/3))"

B.7. Lubkin Sphere-Sphere Contact—Convergence Test

See Section 2.7 for problem description.
   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # Lubkin contact of 2 half-spheres test
 #
   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # Aprepro default mesh value to help FCT
   #{mesh="2"}
 #
 # Aprepro variable settings that are passed in from the test script
 # shape: {shape}
 # mesh: {mesh}
 # elem_topo: {elem_topo}
 # formulation: {formulation}
 # strain_incrementation: {strain_incrementation}
 # material_model: {material_model}
 # torque_tolerance: {torque_tolerance}
 # contact type : {contactType}
 # Torque force flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # reaction_forces: {reaction_forces=0}
 # contact_forces: {contact_forces=1}
 #
 # Aprepro variable settings that are common to all analyses
 # Original load level for preload was 0.0020 -- insufficient to use contact region
 # For mesh 1: first "circular" mesh ring is in contact at a load level of 0.018 (to 2 digits)
 # For mesh 2: first "circular" mesh ring is in contact at a load level of 0.022 (to 2 digits)
 # For mesh 3: first "circular" mesh ring is in contact at a load level of 0.02? (to 2 digits)
 # Load level of 0.023 could give consistent contact with all three meshes -- not checked yet.
 # preload_termination_time: {preload_termination_time = 0.024}
 # Use increment of 0.01 to examine transition from stick to slip
 # twist_time_increment: {twist_time_increment = 0.01}
 # twist_termination_time: {twist_termination_time =
 preload_termination_time + twist_time_increment}
 # For node-face contact (using either a fully integrated or mean-quadrature integrated element)
 # we could obtain convergence in 20 step per time block. Face-face contact with the fully-integrated
 # element forced the smaller time stepping. 40 steps worked for face-face mean-quadrature.
 # With 60 steps for preload, face-full-mesh2 run only made it to step 45.
 # preload_number_steps: {preload_number_steps = 30}
 # 60 steps are not sufficient for face-face contact with fully-integrated hex
 # twist_number_steps: {twist_number_steps = 30}
 # epsilon_time is an offset from the termination_time used to ignore all results
 # except those associated with the final time step.
 # epsilon_time: {epsilon_time = twist_time_increment/(twist_number_steps*2)}
   # Ey: {Ey = 100000.0}
   # nu: {nu = 0.2}
   # R: {R = 4.0}
   # G: {G = 41666.66667}
 # Constants used in Pades Expression
   # a0: {a0 = 0}
   # a1: {a1 = 5.3333}
   # a2: {a2 = 6.0327}
   # a3: {a3 = 19.6951}
   # a4: {a4 = 42.5359}
   # b0: {b0 = 1}
   # b1: {b1 = 5.1193}
   # b2: {b2 = 15.6833}
   # b3: {b3 = 30.8099}
   # b4: {b4 = 72.2111}
 # Absolute rotations of each half sphere, dummy values for now
   # at: {at = 0}
   # ab: {ab = 0}
 # Frictions coefficient used when contact occurs
   # mu: {mu = 0.30}

 341

# Procedure type -- initially only applied to twist loading and only
# defined in the input file for preliminary parameter studies
# proc_type: {proc_type = "adagio"}
# Turning on the next variable reveals a code error as of 7/24/13.
# Once resolved this variable and corresponding aprepro variables
# can be removed.
# extrapolate: {extrapolate = 0}

#----------------------------------------------------------------------

begin sierra Lubkin

# Geometric entities used to define a "rotational displacement field"--

 define point origin with coordinates 0.0 0.0 0.0
 define direction y_direction with vector 0.0 1.0 0.0
 define axis cylindrical_z_axis with point origin direction y_direction

# Functions -----------------------------------------------------------

 # Linear time function (during first time period) for normal displacement
 begin function delta_preload

 expression variable: time = global time
 evaluate expression = "(time<{preload_termination_time}) ? time : {preload_termination_time}"
 end function delta_preload

 # Linear time function (during second time period) for rotational (torsional) displacement
 begin function theta_twist

 expression variable: time = global time
 evaluate expression = "(time>{preload_termination_time}) ? time-{preload_termination_time} : 0.0"
 end function theta_twist

 # This approach did not work in contact.
 # Shifted Heaviside function to turn friction on after the normal preloading
 # begin function friction_switch
 # type is analytic
 # expression variable: time = global time
 # evaluate expression = "(time>{preload_termination_time}) ? 1.0 : 0.0"
 # end function friction_switch

 # y-component of Torque due to one node on the top sphere cut-plane (actually any y-plane)
 # y-component of the vector cross product that yields Torque (F cross x).
 # Use of coordinates (not model_coordinates) => correctly attaining torque in the deformed configuration.
 # This funciton assumes that the center of the cut plane is at x,z=0.
 # Aprepro variable torque_force defines which forces are used to calculate the torque.
 begin function node_torque

 expression variable: x1 = nodal coordinates(x)
 expression variable: x3 = nodal coordinates(z)
 {ifdef(reaction_forces)}
 expression variable: F1 = nodal reaction(x)
 expression variable: F3 = nodal reaction(z)
 # assumes reaction force on the body not on the "restraining plane"
 evaluate expression = "(x3*F1-x1*F3)"
 {endif}
 {ifdef(contact_forces)}
 expression variable: F1 = nodal force_contact(x)
 expression variable: F3 = nodal force_contact(z)
 # using top sphere => sign change but usable with sphere on plane case
 evaluate expression = "(x3*F1-x1*F3)"
 {endif}
 end function node_torque

# material data -------------------------------------------------------

 begin material Mat_1

 342

 density = 1.0
 begin parameters for model {material_model}
 youngs modulus = {Ey}
 poissons ratio = {nu}
 end parameters for model {material_model}
 end material Mat_1

 begin material Mat_2
 density = 1.0
 begin parameters for model {material_model}
 youngs modulus = {Ey}
 poissons ratio = {nu}
 end parameters for model {material_model}
 end material Mat_2

# section data (required to create non-default hex elements) ---------

 # strain incrementation = {strain_incrementation}
 formulation = {formulation}

# FE model ------------------------------------------------------------

 begin finite element model Half-spheres_mesh
 Database Name = {shape}_{elem_topo}_{mesh}.g
 Database Type = exodusII

 material Mat_1
 model = {material_model}
 section = solid_section
 {ifdef(formulation == "mean_quadrature")}
 linear bulk viscosity = 0.06
 quadratic bulk viscosity = 1.20
 hourglass stiffness = 0.05
 hourglass viscosity = 0.0
 {endif}

 material Mat_2
 model = {material_model}
 section = solid_section
 {ifdef(formulation == "mean_quadrature")}
 linear bulk viscosity = 0.06
 quadratic bulk viscosity = 1.20
 hourglass stiffness = 0.05
 hourglass viscosity = 0.0
 {endif}

 end finite element model Half-spheres_mesh

  # analytical solution for the contact radius -- used mainly initially to judge the adequacy of the mes

  compute global mesh from expression "{mesh}"

 # Solution verification

B.12. Oscillating Block Spring With Friction

See Section 2.12 for problem description.
 begin sierra oscillating_block_spring_with_friction

 # Aprepro macros for problem parameters
 # mass in kg
 # {m = 4}
 # Natural frequency prescribed as 2Pi radians/sec (i.e., 1 hz)
   # {omegan = 2*PI}
 # spring stiffness in N/m to yield prescribed omegan: m*omegan^2
   # {k = m*omegan^2}
 # gravitational constant, 9.81 m/sec^2
 # {g = 9.81}
 # Uinitial ~ offeset of the block, 1 m to the left
   # {Uinitial = -1.0}
 # Coefficient of friction -- defined to produce stick after two cycles of oscillation
   # mu = -k*Uinitial/(9*g*m) = 400 Pi^2/8829 ~ 4.47144836216268e-1
   # {mu = -k*Uinitial/(9*g*m)}
 # Ud ~ a measure of the decrease in peak displacement that occurs linearly in time: mu*g/omegan^2
   # {Ud = mu*g/omegan^2}

 {include("func_matl_femodel.i")}

 begin time stepping block preload
 start time = 0.0

 time step scale factor = 0.95
 end
 end
 begin time stepping block free_vibration
 start time = 10.0

 time step scale factor = 0.95
 end
 end
 termination time = 20
 end

 begin presto region presto_region

 {include("region_shared.i")}

 search = dash

 surfaces = surf_1 surf_2
 friction model = mod

 begin time variant model mod
 model = frictionless during periods preload
 model = fric during periods free_vibration
 end

 # coefficient defined such that stick occurs after 2 cycles of vibration

 end

B.13. Friction Wedge

See Section 2.13 for problem description.
 BEGIN SIERRA friction_wedge

 TITLE frictional wedge with rigid body contact

 # Pedigree: This test is related to two other verification/performance tests.
 # (1) rigid_body_wedge (path: adagio_rtest/presto/rigid_body_wedge).
 # This test was used as the initial template for friction_wedge. It shares
 # the same mesh and much of the same input, but the emphasis of friction_wedge
 # is upon examining the stick-slip behavior. This one uses rigid body edge
 # wedges.
 # (2) p015_wedge_friction (path: adagio_rtest/performance/p015_wedge_friction)
 # This test shares the same geometry as this test, but the mesh is much finer.
 # Both had been used to study the frictional contact algorithms in the past.
 # This one uses elastic edge wedges.

   ################################################################################

 # Aprepro macros
 # Offset from the critical coefficient of friction of 0.2.
 # Original offset was 0.001 but up to 0.1 was also examined.
   # {fric_offset = 0.001}
 # time to transition to a constant velocity. Used as the interval for deacceleration too.
   # {time_Vconst = 0.001}
 # time to start transition to zero velocity, i.e., where constant velocity ends
   # {time_Deaccel = 0.002}
 # time to end analysis, end of constant position time interval, std value is 0.004
   # {time_end = 0.05}
 # maximum velocity magnitude. standard value = 25 in/sec
 # {velocity_max = 5}
 # Number of momentum balance iterations
 # momentum_bal_iter now passed in via the command line

 ##### INITIALIZE DIRECTIONS
 DEFINE DIRECTION Y WITH VECTOR 0.0 1.0 0.0

 # The following function is used to displace the top wedge downward and the bottom block upward
 # with a velocity boundary condition. It consists of a cosine transtion from zero
 # to unity, an interval of unity, a cosine transition back down to zero
 # velocity (fixed position), and then a fixed position for the remainder of time.
 begin function cosine2unity2zero

            0.0                       "(1.0-cos(t*PI/{time_Vconst}))/2.0"
            {time_Vconst}             "1.0"
            {time_Deaccel}              "(cos((t-{time_Deaccel})*PI/{time_Vconst})+1.0)/2.0"
 {time_Deaccel+time_Vconst} "0.0"
 end

   ################################################################################

 ##### DEFINE MATERIALS

 BEGIN material edge_wedge_mat
         DENSITY = 7.4e-5
 BEGIN PARAMETERS FOR MODEL ELASTIC
 YOUNGS MODULUS = 1.0e4
 POISSONS RATIO = 0.00
 END PARAMETERS FOR MODEL ELASTIC
 END material edge_wedge_mat

 BEGIN material center_wedge_mat
         DENSITY = 7.4e-5

 366

 BEGIN PARAMETERS FOR MODEL ELASTIC
 YOUNGS MODULUS = 1.0E4
 POISSONS RATIO = 0.00
 END PARAMETERS FOR MODEL ELASTIC
 END material center_wedge_mat

 END RESULTS OUTPUT OUTPUT_PRESTO

 block = block_2

B.16. Hex Patch Tests—Quasi-Static, Linear Elastic

See Section 3.1 for problem description.
 begin sierra Hex_patch

 # Units
 # length: inches
 # force: lbs
 # stress: psi
 # time: seconds (pseudo-time for these quasistatic runs)

     # Aprepro macros ..............
     # {u_factor = 1.0E-4}
     # {final_time = 1.0}
 # {number_steps = 2}
 # Expected normal and shear stresses expected from the patch test -- a linear elastic approximation
 # Note that these could be obtained more symbolically to generalize the test.
     # {sigNormal = 400.0}
     # {sigShear =   80.0}
 # epsilon_time is an off set from the final_time used to ignore all results
 # except those associated with the final time step.
 # { epsilon_time = final_time/(number_steps*2)}

 # Required externally provided macros with string values:
 # normal_stress_tolerance
 # shear_stress_tolerance
 # strain_incrementation
 # formulation
 # meshFile
 # material model
 # Required externally provided macros "on" or "off":
 # mean_quad
 # selective_dev

 # Ux displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function u
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression is "t*{u_factor}*(2.0*x+y+z)"
 end function u

 # Uy displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function v
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression is "t*{u_factor}*(x+2.0*y+z)"
 end function v

 # Uz displacement component as a function of space and time

 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression = "t*{u_factor}*(x+y+2.0*z)"
 end function w

 # Lame constants

 # Expected normal and shear stresses expected from the patch test
 # These forms could be simplified, but in this "pass" we are pasting them from Matematica and minor changes

begin sierra Hex_patch

 # Units
 # length: inches
 # force: lbs
 # stress: psi
 # time: seconds (pseudo-time for these quasistatic runs)

 # Ux displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function u
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression is "t*{u_factor}*(2.0*x+y+z)"
 end function u

 # Uy displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function v
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression is "t*{u_factor}*(x+2.0*y+z)"
 end function v

 # Uz displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function w
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression = "t*{u_factor}*(x+y+2.0*z)"
 end function w

  start time = {final_time-epsilon_time}
  at step 1 increment = 1
  termination time = {final_time}
  # global time -- unnecessary with spyhis format
  global sigiierr
  global normal_stress_tolerance
  global sigijerr
  global shear_stress_tolerance
end heartbeat output stress_tabular_output

  skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}
  completion file = VerifSigNormal
  verify global sig11err = 0.0
  verify global sig22err = 0.0
  verify global sig33err = 0.0
  tolerance = {normal_stress_tolerance}
end

  skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}
  completion file = VerifSigShear
  verify global sig12err = 0.0
  verify global sig13err = 0.0
  verify global sig23err = 0.0
  tolerance = {shear_stress_tolerance}
end

{if(formulation=="q1p0")}

{Else}

  skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}
  completion file = VerifUSigNormal
  verify global usig11err = 0.0
  verify global usig22err = 0.0
  verify global usig33err = 0.0
  tolerance = {normal_stress_tolerance}
end

  skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}
  completion file = VerifUSigShear
  verify global usig12err = 0.0
  verify global usig13err = 0.0
  verify global usig23err = 0.0
  tolerance = {shear_stress_tolerance}
end

{Endif}

 completion file = VerifVNormal
 verify global V11err = 0.0
 verify global V22err = 0.0
 verify global V33err = 0.0
 tolerance = {normal_stretch_tolerance}
 end

 skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}

 verify global V12err = 0.0
 verify global V13err = 0.0

 end adagio region adagio_patch_region

B.21. Hex Convergence Test—Cantilever Beam

See Section 3.6 for problem description.
 # Set default for the mesh to help FCT
 {ifndef(mesh)}
   #{mesh="2"}
 {endif}
   # ---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #
 # Aprepro Variable Settings
 # mesh: {mesh}
 # formulation: {formulation}
 # Formulation flags: 0~off, 1~on
 # mean_quad: {mean_quad}
 # selective_dev: {selective_dev}
 # strain_incrementation: {strain_incrementation}
 # material_model: {material_model}
 # elem_topo: {elem_topo}
   #---------------------------------------------------------------------
 #

 begin Sierra slender_beam_example

 # slender beam example -- constant shear load
 #
 # Cantilever support at negative-x end with
 # transverse shear load at positive-x end
 # use length-to-depth ratio of 10

     # Loading function ----------------------------------------------------

 begin function load
 type = piecewise linear
 ordinate = shear
 abscissa = time

 1.0 1.0

 end function load

   # material data -------------------------------------------------------

 begin material beam_stuff
       density          = 1.0E-2
 begin parameters for model {material_model}
 youngs modulus = 1.0E6
 poissons ratio = 0.3
 end parameters for model {material_model}
 end material beam_stuff

 # section data (required to create non-default hex elements) ----------

 strain incrementation = {strain_incrementation}
 formulation = {formulation}
 {Ifdef(selective_dev)}
 deviatoric parameter = 0.5
 {Else}
 # Undefined selective_dev
 {Endif}

 {If(total_lagrange)}
 begin total lagrange section tl_section
 strain incrementation = {strain_incrementation}

 406

 formulation = {formulation}
 volume average j = off
 # Undefined selective_dev
 end total lagrange section tl_section
 {Endif}

# FE model ------------------------------------------------------------

 begin finite element model slender_beam
 database name = beam{mesh}_{elem_topo}.g
 database type = exodusII

 material beam_stuff
 model = {material_model}
 {If(total_lagrange)}
 section = tl_section
 {Else}
 section = solid_section
 {Endif}
 {Ifdef(mean_quad)}
 linear bulk viscosity = 0.06
 quadratic bulk viscosity = 1.20
 hourglass stiffness = 0.05
 hourglass viscosity = 0.0
 {Else}
 # Undefined mean_quad
 {Endif}

 end finite element model slender_beam

# procedure data ------------------------------------------------------

 begin adagio procedure beam_procedure

 start time = 0.0
 begin parameters for adagio region beam_region
 time increment = 0.2
 end parameters for adagio region beam_region

 termination time = 1.0

 begin adagio region beam_region

 use finite element model slender_beam

# BC data -------------------------------------------------------------

# traction load on right end

 surface = right_ss
 Node Set Subroutine = parabolic_Yshear
 Scale factor = 1.0
 end traction

 # traction reaction on left end

 surface = left_ss
 Node Set Subroutine = parabolic_Yshear
 scale factor = -1.0
 end traction

 407

 # displacement bcs on left end to prevent rigid body motions
 # and prescribe fixity with respect to x

 surface = left_ss
 Component = X

 node set = left_y_line
 Component = Y

 node set = left_z_line
 Component = Z

# Output data -------------------------------------------------------------

 end

 begin results output beam_output
 database name = beam{mesh}_Adagio.e
 database type = exodusII
 at step 0 increment = 1
 nodal variables = displacement as displ
 nodal variables = nodal_stress as nodal_stress
 element variables = stress as elem_stress
 nodal variables = force_external as fext
 end results output beam_output

# solver data ---------------------------------------------------------

 begin cg
 target relative residual = 1e-8
# acceptable relative residual = 1e-12
# {Ifdef(mean_quad)}
# target relative residual = 0.06
# {Else}
# target relative residual = 1e-9
# {Endif}
 maximum iterations = 1000

 linear solver = feti
 small number of iterations = 15
 conditioning = no_check
 end
 end

 end adagio region beam_region

 end adagio procedure beam_procedure

 end

end sierra slender_beam_example

B.22. Tet Patch Tests—Quasi-Static, Linear Elastic

See Section 3.7 for problem description.
 begin sierra Tet_patch

 # Units
 # length: inches
 # force: lbs
 # stress: psi
 # time: seconds (pseudo-time for these quasistatic runs)

     # Aprepro macros ..............
     # {u_factor = 1.0E-4}
     # {final_time = 1.0}
 # {number_steps = 2}
 # Expected normal and shear stresses expected from the patch test -- a linear elastic approximation
 # Note that these could be obtained more symbolically to generalize the test.
     # {sigNormal = 400.0}
     # {sigShear =   80.0}
 # epsilon_time is an off set from the final_time used to ignore all results
 # except those associated with the final time step.
 # { epsilon_time = final_time/(number_steps*2)}

 # Required externally provided macros with string values:
 # normal_stress_tolerance
 # shear_stress_tolerance
 # strain_incrementation
 # formulation
 # meshFile
 # material model
 # Required externally provided macros "on" or "off":
 # nodal_tet
 # composite_tet

 # Ux displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function u
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression is "t*{u_factor}*(2.0*x+y+z)"
 end function u

 # Uy displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function v
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression is "t*{u_factor}*(x+2.0*y+z)"
 end function v

 # Uz displacement component as a function of space and time
 begin function w
 type = analytic
 expression variable: x = nodal model_coordinates(1)
 expression variable: y = nodal model_coordinates(2)
 expression variable: z = nodal model_coordinates(3)
 expression variable: t = global time
 evaluate expression = "t*{u_factor}*(x+y+2.0*z)"
 end function w

 begin material block_mat
       density         = 2.61e-4

 409

  begin parameters for model {material_model}
 youngs modulus = 1.0e6
 poissons ratio = 0.25
  end parameters for model {material_model}
end material block_mat

{Ifdef(composite_tet)}
begin total lagrange section solid_1
  formulation = composite_tet
end
{Else}

  strain incrementation = {strain_incrementation}
  formulation = {formulation}
end
{Endif}

  Database Name = {meshFile}
  Database Type = exodusII

 material = block_mat
 model = {material_model}
 section = solid_1

begin adagio procedure adagio_patch_procedure

 begin time stepping block ramp_segment
 start time = 0.0
 begin parameters for adagio region adagio_patch_region
 time increment = {final_time/number_steps}
 end parameters for adagio region adagio_patch_region
 end time stepping block ramp_segment
 termination time = {final_time}

  begin adagio region adagio_patch_region

 ### output description ###

 Database Name = tet_patch.e
 Database Type = exodusII
 At Time 0.0, Increment = {final_time}
 nodal Variables = force_external as f_ext
 nodal Variables = force_internal as f_int
 nodal Variables = displacement
 element Variables = stress as stress_el
 element Variables = log_strain as strain_el
 {Ifdef(nodal_tet)}
 nodal Variables = stress_1 as stress_node
 {Endif}
 element variables = timestep as elem_time_step
 global Variables = timestep as timestep
 global variables = external_energy as ExternalEnergy
 global variables = internal_energy as InternalEnergy

 ### definition of BCs ###

 # X displacements

 410

  node set = nodelist_1 nodelist_2 nodelist_3 nodelist_4 nodelist_5 nodelist_6
  # block = block_1
  component = X
  function = u
  scale factor = 1.0
end

# Y displacements

  node set = nodelist_1 nodelist_2 nodelist_3 nodelist_4 nodelist_5 nodelist_6
  # block = block_1
  component = Y
  function = v
  scale factor = 1.0
end

# Z displacements

  node set = nodelist_1 nodelist_2 nodelist_3 nodelist_4 nodelist_5 nodelist_6
  # block = block_1
  component = Z
  function = w
  scale factor = 1.0
end

# Solver parameters

 type = scale_factor
 scale factor = 0.0
  end
  begin cg
 target relative residual = 1.0e-11
 {Ifndef(nodal_tet)}
 maximum iterations = 20 # Reasonable for tangent preconditioner

 linear solver = feti # explicitly putting this line for feature coverage tool.
 conditioning = no_check
 end
 {Else}
 # Parameters for the nodal-based tet
 # These parameters from adagio/tet4n_uni_disp_cube_thermal
 maximum iterations = 20
 orthogonality measure for reset = 0.1
 line search secant
 preconditioner = elastic
 {Endif}

  end cg

# Solution verification
# Examine the bounds of the "expected stresses". Note that these stresses are based on
# linear elastic bodies, and thus are not exact for the finite strain formulation.

  block = block_1
  {Ifndef(nodal_tet)}
  compute global sig11max as max of element stress(xx)
  compute global sig11min as min of element stress(xx)
  compute global sig22max as max of element stress(yy)
  compute global sig22min as min of element stress(yy)
  compute global sig33max as max of element stress(zz)
  compute global sig33min as min of element stress(zz)
  compute global sig12max as max of element stress(xy)

 411

 compute global sig12min as min of element stress(xy)
 compute global sig13max as max of element stress(xz)
 compute global sig13min as min of element stress(xz)
 compute global sig23max as max of element stress(yz)
 compute global sig23min as min of element stress(yz)

 {Else}
 compute global sig11max as max of nodal stress_1(xx)
 compute global sig11min as min of nodal stress_1(xx)
 compute global sig22max as max of nodal stress_1(yy)
 compute global sig22min as min of nodal stress_1(yy)
 compute global sig33max as max of nodal stress_1(zz)
 compute global sig33min as min of nodal stress_1(zz)
 compute global sig12max as max of nodal stress_1(xy)
 compute global sig12min as min of nodal stress_1(xy)
 compute global sig13max as max of nodal stress_1(xz)
 compute global sig13min as min of nodal stress_1(xz)
 compute global sig23max as max of nodal stress_1(yz)
 compute global sig23min as min of nodal stress_1(yz)
 {Endif}
 # Relative percent error in each stress component
 compute global sig11err from expression "max(abs(sig11max-{sigNormal}),abs(sig11min-{sigNormal}))*100/
 compute global sig22err from expression "max(abs(sig22max-{sigNormal}),abs(sig22min-{sigNormal}))*100/
 compute global sig33err from expression "max(abs(sig33max-{sigNormal}),abs(sig33min-{sigNormal}))*100/
 compute global sig12err from expression "max(abs(sig12max-{sigShear} ),abs(sig12min-{sigShear} ))*100/
 compute global sig13err from expression "max(abs(sig13max-{sigShear} ),abs(sig13min-{sigShear} ))*100/
 compute global sig23err from expression "max(abs(sig23max-{sigShear} ),abs(sig23min-{sigShear} ))*100/
 end

 skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}
 completion file = VerifNormal
 verify global sig11err = 0.0
 verify global sig22err = 0.0
 verify global sig33err = 0.0
 tolerance = {normal_stress_tolerance}
 end

 skip times = 0.0 to {final_time-epsilon_time}
 completion file = VerifShear
 verify global sig12err = 0.0
 verify global sig13err = 0.0
 verify global sig23err = 0.0
 tolerance = {shear_stress_tolerance}
 end

 end adagio region adagio_patch_region
 end adagio procedure adagio_patch_procedure

 end

 end

end sierra Tet_patch

 #
 #
 # Hat Section:
 # Note, thickness is d4 for all sections
 #
 #
 # d3
   #                      +----------------+ ------------------+-
   #                      |       r2       |                   |
   #                      +--+----------+--+                   |
   #            d4        | |           | |                    |
   #             |        |r1|        ->|r3|<-d4              d2
   #             v        | |           | |                    |
   #         +------------+--+          +--+------------+      |
   #         |      r0       |          |        r4     |      |
   #         +---------------+          +---------------+ -----+-
 # ^
   #         |   |                                      |
   #    t    +-------------------d1---------------------+
 # ^
   #    |
   #    |
 # +--->s
 #
 begin beam section hat
 section = HAT

 452

 d1 = 0.40
 d2 = 0.10
 d3 = 0.15
 d4 = 0.01
 t axis = 0 1 0
 #Visualize Integration Points = on
 end

# d1

#         |             |

 begin beam section bar
 section = BAR
 d1 = 0.1
 d2 = 0.2
 t axis = 0 1 0
 #Visualize Integration Points = on
 end

# d1

#         | |       ->| |<-d3
#         | |         | |
#         | |         | |d2
#         | |         | |
#         | |         | |
#    t    | +---------+ |
#    ^    +-------------+
#    |

 begin beam section box
 section = BOX
 d1 = 0.10

#    |         d1

#
# Analytic Results:
# Problem consists of four beams for each section type.
The beams are organized in a matrix with
# numbering scheme
# block_A00 : Beams of section type A.
# nodelist_ABC : Nodelist on beam section type A, loading condition B, and beam end C
#
#
The four beams of a section type are subjected to pure axial extension, pure T direction bending, pure
# S direction bending, and pure R direction torsion.
#
#
The analytic results are computed with the following mathematica code (betwen the #ifdef and #endif lines.
# In order to run this mathematica code:
# 1) run the ’MATERIAL MODEL SETUP BLOCK’
# 2) Run ONE of the section defintion blocks
# 3) Run the ’RESPONSE EQUATIONS BLOCK’
# 4) Run the ’DATA GATHERING BLOCK’
# 5) run the ’DATA PRINT BLOCK’.
he data print block will output the analytic force response functions which are found
# found below
#
#{ifdef(COMMENT_OUT)}

(*===============================================================================*)
(* MATERIAL MODEL SETUP BLOCK *)
Clear[d1, d2, d3, d5, height, width, y0, ym, stress, x, y, strain, e0];
(* Stress Strain Law. Elastic Perfectly Plastic.
  ym = youngs modulus
  y0 = yield stress

 454

  gm = shear modulus
  g0 = shear yield strength
  beamLen = the physical length of the beams in the mesh *)
(*
Note, here assuming poissons ratio 0.0 which makes the shear modulus one half of youngs modulus *)
beamLen = 2.0;
y0 = 1.0;
ym = 1000000;
g0 = y0/2.0;
gm = ym/2.0;
stressRR[strain_] = If[strain*ym < y0, If[strain*ym > -y0, strain*ym, -y0], y0];
stressST[strain_] = If[strain*gm < g0, If[strain*gm > -g0, strain*gm, -g0], g0];
(*===============================================================================*)

(*===============================================================================*)
(* Specific Parameters for I section *)
Clear[x, y, IStencil, DataI];
SectName = "I";
d1 = 0.15;
d2 = 0.2;
d3 = 0.01;
d4 = 0.015;
height = d2;
width = d1;
(* Use open section analytic J, which is given by sum of section sub lengths B
times t^3 / 3. *)
SumB1 = d1 + d1;
SumB2 = d2 - 2*d4;
J = SumB1 * (d4^3) / 3.0 + SumB2 * (d3^3)/3.0;
(* Use open section analytic Tmax, T = g0 * B * t^2 /2 *)
Tmax = SumB1 * g0 * (d4^2)/2.0 + SumB2*(d3^2)/2.0;
(* I stencil. Returns 1 if X/Y is in the section, Returns 0 if X/Y is not in the section *)

IStencil[x_, y_] = If[y <= d4 || y >= (d2 - d4), 1,
   If[x > d1/2.0 - d3/2.0 && x < d1/2.0 + d3/2.0, 1, 0]];

(* Plot the section to make sure it the stencil is right *)
DataI = {};
For[x = 0.0, x <= width, x += width/200,
  For[y = 0.0, y < height, y += height/200,
 If[IStencil[x, y] > 0, DataI = Append[DataI, {x, y}]]
 ];
  ];
ListPlot[DataI]
SectionStencil = IStencil;
(*===============================================================================*)

(*===============================================================================*)
(* Specific Parameters for Hat section *)
Clear[x, y, HatStencil, DataHat];
SectName = "Hat";
d1 = 0.4;
d2 = 0.1;
d3 = 0.15;
d4 = 0.01;
height = d2;
width = d1;
(*
Engineering Approximation: Use open section analytic J, which is given by sum of section sub lengths B times t
*)
SumB = (2*(d1/2.0 - d3/2.0 + d4/2.0) + 2*(d2 - d4) + (d3 - d4));
J = SumB * (d4^3) / 3.0;
(* Use open section analytic Tmax, T = g0 * B * t^2 /2 *)
Tmax = SumB * g0 * (d4^2)/2.0;
(* Hat stencil. Returns 1 if X/Y is in the section, Returns 0 if X/Y is not in the section *)
HatStencil[x_, y_] =
  If[y <= d4 && (x <= (d1/2.0 - d3/2.0 + d4) ||
      x >= (d1/2.0 + d3/2.0 - d4)), 1,

 455

   If[y >= d4 &&
 y <= d2 -
        d4 && (x >= d1/2.0 - d3/2.0 && x <= (d1/2.0 - d3/2.0) + d4 ||
        x >= d1/2.0 + d3/2.0 - d4 && x <= d1/2.0 + d3/2.0), 1,
 If[y >=
        d2 - d4 && (x >= d1/2.0 - d3/2.0 && x <= d1/2.0 + d3/2.0), 1,
 0]]];
(* Plot the section to make sure it the stencil is right *)
DataHat = {};
For[x = 0.0, x <= width, x += width/200,
  For[y = 0.0, y < height, y += height/200,
 If[HatStencil[x, y] > 0, DataHat = Append[DataHat, {x, y}]]
 ];
  ];
ListPlot[DataHat]
SectionStencil = HatStencil;
(*===============================================================================*)

(*===============================================================================*)
(* Specific Parameters for Bar section *)
Clear[x, y, BarStencil, DataBar];
SectName = "Bar";
d1 = 0.10;
d2 = 0.20;
height = d2;
width = d1;
(* Engineering Approximation: compute J and Tmax based of of the warping correction factors for a 2 by 1 recta
J = 0.229*d2*d1^3;
Tmax = ((g0*d2*d1^2)/4.07)*1.694;
(* Bar stencil. Returns 1 if X/Y is in the section, Returns 0 if X/Y is not in the section *)
BarStencil[x_, y_] = 1.0;
DataBar = {};
For[x = 0.0, x <= width, x += width/200,
  For[y = 0.0, y < height, y += height/200,
 If[BarStencil[x, y] > 0, DataBar = Append[DataBar, {x, y}]]
 ];
  ];
ListPlot[DataBar]
SectionStencil = BarStencil;
(*===============================================================================*)

(*===============================================================================*)
(* Specific Parameters for Rod section *)

Clear[x, y, RodStencil, DataRod];
SectName = "Rod";
d1 = 0.20;
d2 = 0.20;
height = d2;
width = d1;
(* Engineering Approximation: For a circular section under torsion plane sections actually
will remain plane (no warping) analytic solution exists for J and max torision *)
J = Pi * (d1^4)/32;
Tmax = ((2*Pi*(d1/2)^3)/3)*g0;
(* Rod stencil. Returns 1 if X/Y is in the section, Returns 0 if X/Y is not in the section *)
RodStencil[x_, y_] =
  If[(x - d1/2)^2 + (y - d1/2)^2 < (d1/2)^2, 1, 0];
DataRod = {};
For[x = 0.0, x <= width, x += width/100,
  For[y = 0.0, y < height, y += height/100,
 If[RodStencil[x, y] > 0, DataRod = Append[DataRod, {x, y}]]
 ];
  ];
ListPlot[DataRod]
SectionStencil = RodStencil;
(*===============================================================================*)

(*===============================================================================*)

 456

(* Box Section Parameters *)
Clear[x, y, BoxStencil, DataBox];
SectName = "Box";
d1 = 0.10;
d2 = 0.20;
d3 = 0.01;
height = d2;
width = d1;
(* Use closed section analytic J, which is given by J = 4A^2/(B/t). *)
(* Engineering Approximation: Compute J useing closed seciton approximation.
A is the area enclosed by the section midplane, and B is the total section length at the midplane *)
A = (d1 - d3)*(d2 - d3);
B = 2*(d1 - d3) + 2*(d2 - d3);
J = (4*A^2)/(B/d3);
(* Use closed section analytic T = 2*g0*t*A *)
Tmax = 2*g0*d3*A;
(* Box stencil. Returns 1 if X/Y is in the section, Returns 0 if X/Y is not in the section *)
BoxStencil[x_, y_] = If[x > d3 && x < d1 - d3 && y > d3 && y < d2 - d3, 0, 1];
DataBox = {};
For[x = 0.0, x <= width, x += width/200,
  For[y = 0.0, y < height, y += height/200,
 If[BoxStencil[x, y] > 0, DataBox = Append[DataBox, {x, y}]]
 ];
  ];
ListPlot[DataBox]
SectionStencil = BoxStencil;
(*===============================================================================*)

(*===============================================================================*)
(* RESPONSE EQUATIONS BLOCK *)
(* Define equation for force and moment produced by section for a given incremental rotation or extension.
*)
Clear[Mtt, Mss, Frr, Mrr];
(* X moment in a beam for incremental rotation dthet about x at end of beam *)
Mtt[dthet_] := Module[
 {eps, Force, MomentTot, ForceTot, x, y, yc},
 Clear[eps, Force, MomentTot, ForceTot, x, y, yc];
   If[dthet == 0.0, Return[0]];
 (* eps is the strain at a given X/Y point. dthet known,
location of neutral axis, yc is unknown*)
   eps[x_, y_, yc_] = (dthet)*(y - yc);
 (* Stress at a given point in a section. If point is not in the stencil it is zero.
If point is in section the stress is
 given by the stress stain law evaluated at the given strain *)
 Force[x_, y_, yc_] = stressRR[eps[x, y, yc]]*SectionStencil[x, y];
 (* Binary search to find neutral axis.
Find the yc at which the integral of force over the section is zero. *)
 yp0 = 0.0;
 yp1 = height;
 vy0 = Re[ NIntegrate[Force[x, y, yp0], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
 vy1 = Re[ NIntegrate[Force[x, y, yp1], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
   For[i = 0, i < 20, ++i,
       ypm = (yp0 + yp1)/2.0;
 vym = Re[NIntegrate[Force[x, y, ypm], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
       If[(vy0 <= 0 && vym >= 0) || (vy0 >= 0 && vym <= 0),
 yp1 = ypm;
 vy1 = vym;
 Continue[];
 ];
       If[(vym <= 0 && vy1 >= 0) || (vym >= 0 && vy1 <= 0),
 yp0 = ypm;
 vy0 = vym;
 Continue[];
 ];
 ];

 457

   yc = (yp0 + yp1)/2.0;
 (* Return the actual moment assocaited with the solved for neutral axis*)
 MomentTot = NIntegrate[Force[x, y, yc]*y, {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height}];
 Re[MomentTot]
 ];
(* Y moment in a beam for incremental rotation dthet about y at end of beam *)
Mss[dthet_] := Module[
 {eps, Force, MomentTot, ForceTotx, x, y, xc},
 Clear[eps, Force, MomentTot, ForceTot, x, y, xc];
   If[dthet == 0.0, Return[0]];
 (* eps is the strain at a given X/Y point. dthet known,
location of neutral axis, xc is unknown*)
   eps[x_, y_, xc_] = (dthet)*(x - xc);
 (* Stress at a given point in a section. If point is not in the stencil it is zero.
If point is in section the stress is
 given by the stress stain law evaluated at the given strain *)
 Force[x_, y_, xc_] = stressRR[eps[x, y, xc]]*SectionStencil[x, y];
 (* Binary search to find neutral axis.
Find the xc at which the integral of force over the section is zero. *)
 xp0 = 0.0;
 xp1 = width;
 vx0 = Re[NIntegrate[Force[x, y, xp0], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height}, AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
 vx1 = Re[NIntegrate[Force[x, y, xp1], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height}, AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
   For[i = 0, i < 20, ++i,
      xpm = (xp0 + xp1)/2.0;
 vxm = Re[NIntegrate[Force[x, y, xpm], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
      If[(vx0 <= 0 && vxm >= 0) || (vx0 >= 0 && vxm <= 0),
 xp1 = xpm;
 vx1 = vxm;
 Continue[];
 ];
      If[(vxm <= 0 && vx1 >= 0) || (vxm >= 0 && vx1 <= 0),
 xp0 = xpm;
 vx0 = vxm;
 Continue[];
 ];
 ];
   xc = (xp0 + xp1)/2.0;
 (* Return the actual moment assocaited with the solved for neutral axis*)
 MomentTot = NIntegrate[Force[x, y, xc]*x, {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height}];
 Re[MomentTot]
 ];

(* Define equation of force produced by section for a given incremental extension dL/length.
This is for the extension in the RR (length) direction *)
Frr[dL_] := Module[
 {eps, Force, ForceTot, x, y},
 Clear[eps, Force, ForceTot, x, y]
   If[dL == 0.0, Return[0]];
 (* eps is the strain at a given X/Y point.
This is just the uniform value based on the apply dL*)
   eps[x_, y_] = dL;
 (* Stress at a given point in a section. If point is not in the stencil it is zero.
If point is in section the stress is
 given by the stress stain law evaluated at the given strain.
Integrate stress over section to compute total resistance force *)
 Force[x_, y_] = stressRR[eps[x, y]]*SectionStencil[x, y];
 ForceTot = NIntegrate[Force[x, y], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height}];
 ForceTot
 ];
(* R moment in a beam for incremental rotation dthet about r at end of beam.
This is torsional resistance. This equations set assumes the beam does not warp in plane *)
MrrNoWarping[dthet_] := Module[
 {eps, Force, ForceTot, x, y, xc, yc, MomentRR, MomentTot,
dx, dy, dtot, Fxy, Forcex, Forcey},
 Clear[eps, Force, ForceTot, x, y, xc, yc, MomentRR,
MomentTot, dx, dy, dtot, Fxy, Forcex, Forcey, yp0, yp1, vy0, vy1];

 458

     If[dthet == 0.0, Return[0]];
(* Compute strains at an XY point. Location of neutral axis (xc, yc) is unknown. *)
     dx[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = dthet*(yc - y);
     dy[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = dthet*(x - xc);
     dtot[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = Sqrt[dx[x, y, xc, yc]^2 + dy[x, y, xc, yc]^2];
(*
compute shear stress at a given point (Fxy) and force per unit area at a given point (Forcex and Forcey) *)
 Fxy[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = stressST[dtot[x, y, xc, yc]]*SectionStencil[x, y];
   Forcex[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = Fxy [x, y, xc, yc]* (dx[x, y, xc, yc]/dtot[x, y, xc, yc]);
   Forcey[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = Fxy [x, y, xc, yc]* (dy[x, y, xc, yc]/dtot[x, y, xc, yc]);
(* Binary search for location of neutral axis.
The neutral axis is the (xc, yc) point in the beam at which the sum of both the Forcex and Forcey over the sec
(*
Note all current sections tested are symmetric about x, so for all these sections it is known that the neutral
Explicitly plug this in to speed *)
(* up the netural axis calculations.
The only section this may not hold for are L and T1, and C1, will need to change this approximation if evaluat
   xc = width/2.0;
 yp0 = 0.0;
 yp1 = height;
 vy0 = Re[NIntegrate[Forcex[x, y, xc, yp0], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
 vy1 = Re[ NIntegrate[Forcex[x, y, xc, yp1], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
   For[i = 0, i < 20, ++i,
       ypm = (yp0 + yp1)/2.0;
 vym = Re[NIntegrate[ Forcex[x, y, xc, ypm], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height},
AccuracyGoal -> 8]];
       If[(vy0 <= 0 && vym >= 0) || (vy0 >= 0 && vym <= 0),
 yp1 = ypm;
 vy1 = vym;
 Continue[];
 ];
       If[(vym <= 0 && vy1 >= 0) || (vym >= 0 && vy1 <= 0),
 yp0 = ypm;
 vy0 = vym;
 Continue[];
 ];
 ];
   yc = (yp0 + yp1)/2.0;
(* Integrate to compute the actual moment assocaited with the solved for refernce axis. *)
   MomentRR[x_, y_, xc_, yc_] = (x - xc)*Forcey[x, y, xc, yc] - (y - yc)*
Forcex[x, y, xc, yc];
 MomentTot = NIntegrate[ MomentRR[x, y, xc, yc], {x, 0, width}, {y, 0, height}];
 Re[MomentTot]
 ];
(*
Calculate torsion based on engineering assumptions, I.e., known elastic J and max torsion resitance Tmax. *)
MrrEnginneeringApprox[dthet_] := Module[
 {Melastic, MomentTot},
 Clear[Melastic, MomentTot];
 (*
Compute the elastic moment, if this is less than the maximum plastic moment use it, othersise use max plastic
 Melastic = J*dthet*gm;
 If[Melastic < Tmax, MomentTot = Melastic, MomentTot = Tmax];
 MomentTot
 ];
(*===============================================================================*)
